L1651: ## 6. Combined Review for In vitro Studies
L1652: ## 6.1. Plasma Protein Binding
L1653: ## Study ADME2
L1654: **Title:** Determination of Free SA Concentration In Rat, Mouse and Human Serum by Ultrafiltration
L1655: **Method:** Samples were incubated with ^{3}\text{H}-SA and ^{14}\text{C}-Glucose and were then subjected to centrifugal ultrafiltration. The percentage of free SA was estimated by dividing the ratio of ^{3}\text{H}-SA and ^{14}\text{C}-Glucose concentration in the ultrafiltrate by the ratio of the ^{3}\text{H}-SA and ^{14}\text{C}-Glucose in the retentate.
L1656: **Result:** About 4.42% (SE 0.368) of SA was free (not protein bound) in human serum.
L1657: ## Study SA® General Pharmacology 1
L1658: **Title:** Relative Binding Affinity of SA to Steroid Hormone Receptors and Sex Hormone Binding Globulin (SHBG)
L1659: **Method:** Third trimester pregnancy serum was used as the source of SHBG and ^{3}\text{H}-testosterone was used as the radioactive ligand. Dextran-charcoal was used to separate bound and free radioactivity.
L1660: **Result:** Levonorgestrel and desogestrel bound to SHBG with significant binding affinity. The binding of SA and progesterone to SHBG was negligible.
L1661: ## 6.2. In Vitro Metabolism
L1662: ## Study XT174097
L1663: **Title:** In vitro cytochrome P450 reaction phenotyping of SA in human liver microsomes and recombinant human CYP enzymes
L1664: **Method:** In vitro clearance of SA (based on loss of parent test drug) in incubations with a panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes (namely, CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 3A4, and 3A5), and with pooled human liver microsomes in the absence and presence of selective inhibitors of the aforementioned CYP enzymes.
L1665: **Result:** Incubations of SA (0.5 \mu\text{M}) in the presence of selective metabolism-dependent inhibitors for various CYP enzymes further supported CYP3A4-mediated metabolism of SA. SA clearance was significantly diminished in the presence of the metabolism-dependent CYP3A4/5 inhibitor troleandomycin (71.8% inhibition) and the selective CYP3A4 inhibitor CYP3cide (84.6% inhibition). The contributions of other CYP enzymes to the clearance of SA were less than 20%. The contribution of CYP3A4 to the microsomal clearance of SA based on the degree of
L1666: inhibition by CYP3cide (~85%) was comparable to that previously estimated from the degree of inhibition by the CYP3A4/5 inhibitor ketoconazole (87%).
L1667: **Conclusion:** CYP3A4 is the major contributor (~85%) to the metabolic clearance of SA by human liver microsomes.
L1668: ## 6.3. Induction/Inhibition of Drug-Metabolizing Enzymes
L1669: ## Study XT103116
L1670: **Title:** In Vitro Evaluation of SA as an Inducer of Cytochrome P450 Expression in Cultured Human Hepatocytes
L1671: **Method:** Three preparations of cultured human hepatocytes from three separate livers were treated once daily for three consecutive days with dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO, 0.1% v/v, vehicle control), one of three concentrations of SA (10, 100 or 1000 nM) or one of three known human CYP inducers, namely, omeprazole, phenobarbital and rifampin. After treatment, the cells were incubated in situ with the appropriate marker substrates for the analysis of phenacetin O-dealkylation (marker for CYP1A2), bupropion hydroxylation (marker for CYP2B6), amodiaquine N-dealkylation (marker for CYP2C8), diclofenac 4'-hydroxylation (marker for CYP2C9), S-mephentytoin 4'-hydroxylation (marker for CYP2C19) and testosterone 6\beta-hydroxylation (marker for CYP3A4/5) by LC/MS/MS.
L1672: **Result:** Treatment of cultured human hepatocytes with up to 1000 nM SA final concentration, caused little or no change in CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19 and 3A4/5 activity (&lt;2.0-fold increase and less than 8% of the effect caused by the appropriate positive control)
L1673: **Conclusion:** At up to 1000 nM, SA did not induce CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19 and 3A4/5.
L1674: ## Study XT105121
L1675: **Title:** In Vitro Evaluation of SA as an Inhibitor of Cytochrome P450 (CYP) Enzymes in Human Liver Microsomes
L1676: **Method:** Human liver microsomes from a pool of 16 individuals were incubated with marker substrates, at concentrations approximately equal to Km or S50, in the presence or absence of SA. To evaluate time- and metabolism-dependent inhibition, SA was preincubated with human liver microsomes in the absence and presence of an NADPH-generating system, respectively, for 30 minutes prior to incubation with the marker substrate. Known direct and metabolism-dependent inhibitors of CYP enzymes were included as positive controls.
L1677: **Result:** Under the experimental conditions examined, there was little or no evidence of direct or time-dependent inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1 by SA. At the highest concentration tested (i.e., 1000 nM) and above, SA
L1678: may have the potential to cause metabolism-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4/5 (as measured by midazolam 1'-hydroxylation) as an increase in inhibition (26%) was observed upon preincubation in the presence of NADPH.
L1679: **Conclusion:** Considering the therapeutic exposure levels of SA/EE CVS are less than 5 nM at the most, SA is unlikely to affect the PK of other drugs via CYP inhibition.
L1680: ## Study XT185010
L1681: **Title:** In Vitro Evaluation of Metabolites of SA as Inhibitors of Cytochrome P450 Enzymes in Human Liver Microsomes
L1682: **Method:** The ability of 5\alpha-dihydro-SA (M3) or 17\alpha-hydroxy-5\alpha-dihydro-SA (M6) to inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP3A4 was investigated using a pool of 200 individual human liver microsomal samples. The inhibitory potency of M3 and M6 was determined in vitro by measuring the activity of each CYP enzyme in the presence and absence of test article. The concentration of inhibitor that causes 50% inhibition of marker substrate activity (IC50 value) for direct and time- and metabolism-dependent inhibition of each human CYP enzyme were examined.
L1683: ## Result:

L1693: ## 7. Literature Review of SA Lactation Studies
L1694: The SA/EE 150/15 CVS has not been evaluated in lactating women and CHCs are not recommended in this population. There are 4 publications that evaluated the effect of SA in lactation. Based on the relevance to the current submission, two studies of SA contraceptive implants in nursing women were reviewed below.
L1695: ## 7.1. Article 1: Contraceptive efficacy and clinical performance of SA implants in postpartum women12
L1696: **Method:** blood and milk samples were obtained in a subset of 14 SA implant users who volunteered for this procedure. Blood samples (5 mL) were drawn from the contralateral arm to the implant site, once per week for the first month after insertion of the implant and once every other week thereafter, until weaning. Milk samples (10 mL) were collected by manual compression in the same subjects, immediately after blood samples were obtained. Concentrations of SA in serum and milk were measured by a validated radioimmunoassay.
L1697: ## **Result:**
L1698: SA levels rose in serum to mean concentrations of 175 pmol/liter during the first month after implant insertion (Figure 22). Later on, serum SA decreased progressively reaching mean levels of 60 pmol/liter at the end of the first year. SA concentrations in milk followed a similar pattern to those in serum, and the highest and lowest concentrations were 135 pmol/liter and 54 pmol/liter, respectively. With few exceptions, the milk/serum ratio was under 1. The estimated dose transferred to the infant, assuming a daily intake of 800 mL of milk, is around 50 ng/day in the first month after insertion decreasing thereafter to around 20 ng/day.
L1699: 12 Massai, M. R.; Diaz, S.; Quinteros, E.; Reyes, M. V.; Herreros, C.; Zepeda, A.; Croxatto, H. B.; Moo-Young, A. J., Contraceptive efficacy and clinical performance of Nestorone implants in postpartum women. *Contraception* 2001, 64 (6), 369-76.
L1700: Figure 22. SA levels in serum and milk of lactating women in the first year of use of a single SA implant and SA milk/serum ratio (open circle: plasma level, closed circle: milk levels)
L1701: ## 7.2. Article 2: Use of a Single Implant of Elcometrine (ST-1435), a Nonorally Active Progestin, as a Long Acting Contraceptive for Postpartum Nursing Women13
L1702: **Method:** Blood and milk samples were obtained from the mother before implant insertion as a control. After implant insertion, blood samples were drawn at 15 and 75 days of implant use. Breast milk was obtained at 75 days of implant use. A blood sample was drawn from the baby in the third month of the study, when the baby was 4 months old and the mother had been using the implant for 75 days. Concentrations of SA in serum and milk were measured by a validated radioimmunoassay.
L1703: ## Result:
L1704: SA concentrations in the blood and milk of the lactating women indicated that SA passes freely from maternal blood into milk with a milk to serum ratio of approximately 1 (Figure 23). However, its concentration in the infants' blood (\sim 19 pmol/L) was only about 5% of the mean levels measured in the mothers' blood, likely due to extensive first pass metabolism.
L1705: Figure 23. Levels of SA (Elcometrine) in serum and breast milk of mothers and in serum of infants (mean \pm SE, n = 25)
L1706: 13 Coutinho, E. M.; Athayde, C.; Dantas, C.; Hirsch, C.; Barbosa, L., Use of a single implant of elcometrine (ST-1435), a nonorally active progestin, as a long acting contraceptive for postpartum nursing women. *Contraception* 1999, 59 (2), 115-22.

L1715: ## Memorandum
L1716: ## PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY
## SECONDARY REVIEW MEMO
L1717: Date:	July 18, 2018
NDA #	209627
Sponsor:	The Population Council, Inc.
Drug:	Segesterone acetate/Ethinyl estradiol Contraceptive Vaginal System (SA/EE CVS)
Indication:	Contraception
Reviewer:	Kimberly Hatfield, PhD
Division:	Division of Bone, Reproductive and Urologic Products
L1718: ## **Background:**
L1719: The Population Council has submitted NDA 209627 for an intravaginal ring containing segesterone acetate and ethinyl estradiol for the indication of contraception. Segesterone acetate (SA) is a novel synthetic progestin, and a new chemical entity. Ethinyl estradiol (EE) is a known synthetic estrogen, and has been previously approved for use in several contraceptive and non-contraceptive products. The current application was submitted as a 505(b)(2) application, relying on published scientific literature to support the nonclinical safety of ethinyl estradiol. Nonclinical studies were only conducted to establish the nonclinical safety of segesterone acetate, and to establish the safety of the to-be-marketed device.
L1720: Intravaginal rings have recently been termed by the Agency as 'vaginal systems', and due to the clinical indication, this drug/device product is termed a contraceptive vaginal system (CVS). This CVS is designed for continuous daily release of SA (150 µg/day) and EE (15 µg/day) for a maximum of 13 menstrual cycles of 28 days each. The product is unique in that it is reusable; it is inserted at the beginning of each menstrual cycle, remaining in place for 21 days, removed and stored for a 7-day drug-free interval, and then re-inserted for the subsequent cycle.
L1721: A comprehensive nonclinical package was submitted to support the pharmacology and toxicology of SA at the proposed dose and duration. Relevant published literature was submitted to support the pharmacology and toxicology of EE at the proposed dose and duration. Toxicology studies to evaluate the SA/EE combination were not specifically conducted for this application. The chronic safety of SA and EE alone had already been evaluated via a 12-month monkey study using SA implants, and via literature reference, respectively. EE also has a long history of chronic use in approved drug products with vaginal administration. The local intravaginal safety of the proposed ring containing SA alone was evaluated in a 4-week monkey study, with only expected pharmacodynamic effects observed, and no local toxic effects. In addition, clinical studies informed the PK for the intravaginal use of the SA/EE CVS, eliminating the need for a nonclinical study of chronic duration with the combination. To assess the biocompatibility of the drug/device
L1722: combination, nonclinical studies were completed using extracts from the proposed SA/EE CVS to test the intravaginal safety of the to-be-marketed product. These studies were reviewed by CDRH, and were deemed sufficient to evaluate the local toxicity of the combined drug/device product.
L1723: ## **Summary of nonclinical data:**
L1724: **Pharmacology:** SA demonstrated high binding affinity for the progesterone receptor, with agonist effects. SA did not bind to the estrogen or androgen receptor, or sex hormone binding globulin. Following administration to female rabbits, SA transformed the uterine endothelium from a proliferative to a secretory state and also blocked fertilization. In female rats, SA blocked spontaneous ovulation and prevented pregnancy. SA did not induce any cardiovascular, central nervous system, or respiratory safety pharmacology effects.
L1725: EE binds the estrogen receptor to promote the proliferation and growth of the vaginal, uterine and mammary epithelium, among other effects.
L1726: **Toxicology:** Pivotal studies to evaluate the toxicity of SA were conducted in mice, rats, rabbits and monkeys, and used various routes of administration – vaginal gel, subdermal implant, and vaginal ring – and durations – 4 weeks, 3-months, and 12 months. These studies with SA primarily produced expected pharmacologic effects of a progestin (disruption of estrus cycle, vaginal morphology changes, inhibition of ovulation, mammary growth, decreased reproductive organ weights). One of the 3-month studies was also conducted in male rats, with SA subdermal implants causing decreased testosterone and luteinizing hormone levels, decreased reproductive organ weights and increased adrenal weights.
L1727: SA was not genotoxic following the standard battery of testing (Ames assay, chromosome aberration test in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, and micronucleus assay in mice).
L1728: 2-year carcinogenicity studies were conducted in mice and rats. A dose of 30 mg/kg/day SA (vaginal gel formulation) in mice increased the incidence of adenocarcinoma and lobular hyperplasia in the breast. All doses of SA gel (3, 10, 30 mg/kg/d) increased the incidence of subchronic or chronic inflammation in the vagina. In rats, no evidence of a tumorigenic effect was observed at any dose of SA via administration by subdermal implant (40, 100, 200 µg/d; 267, 667, 1333 µg/kg/day). Only pharmacologic effects were observed. As such, SA was considered carcinogenic in female mice at 30 mg/kg/day, but not in rats. SA was also considered an irritant at all doses in mice. Due to use of subdermal implants, irritation was not evaluated in rats.
L1729: Female rats treated for 3 months with SA via subdermal implant demonstrated a return to fertility 7 weeks after implants were removed. Return of cyclicality, number of corpora lutea and number of implantations were then comparable to controls. An embryofetal development study in rats dosed with SA (GD6-15; up to 250 µg/kg/day) showed no maternal toxicity, embryotoxicity or teratogenicity. An embryofetal development study in rabbits dosed with SA (subcutaneous; up to 250 µg/kg/day) showed an increase in embryofetal death, increased resorptions, and increased vertebral and rib malformations at the high dose of 250 µg/kg/day. In rabbits, the maternal NOAEL was 250 µg/kg, while the fetal NOAEL was 50 µg/kg/day.

L1733: SA: Segesterone acetate; NOAEL: No observed adverse effect level; AUC: Area under the curve; BSA: body surface area; EFD: embryofetal development toxicity study; PPND: perinatal and postnatal development toxicity study
L1734: Multiples calculated based on a clinical daily dose of SA=150 µg/day; 0.0025 mg/kg/day; AUC=14.99 ng*hr/mL
L1735: In a prenatal and postnatal development study in rats (subcutaneous SA up to 250 µg/kg/day; GD15-PND21), SA was found to prolong gestation and parturition. In dams that had not delivered or partially delivered by GD25, delivered pups were dead or stillborn, while excised uteri showed dead fetuses in animals that had partially delivered. Unscheduled deaths in the vehicle, mid and high dose were due to prolonged gestation and delivery complications. At the mid and high dose (50 and 250 µg/kg/day), there was significant maternal mortality during parturition, increased parturition, increased perinatal pup deaths, and only 2/25 high dose rats delivered live pups. The maternal and fetal NOEL was determined to be &lt;2 µg/kg/day.
L1736: The toxicity of EE was provided in published literature reports. The acute toxic dose for EE in rodents is 0.5-5 g/kg (liver and kidney injury) and repeat dose studies primarily induce pharmacologic effects in female reproductive organs (uterine hypertrophy, vaginal keratinization, mammary gland development) with toxic effects observed in the liver and hematopoietic tissues at supra-physiological doses. EE is not mutagenic via the Ames assay, but is positive in mammalian cell chromosomal aberration assays. EE can act as a tumor promoter. At doses up to 4 mg/kg/day in rats for 2 years, EE did not increase the incidence of tumors or tumor development, but altered the incidence of tumor types, namely hepatocellular adenomas, uterine polyps, pituitary adenomas and mammary gland tumors. This is currently noted in class labeling for estrogens. In the reproductive system, EE prevents reproduction either alone or in combination with progestins.
L1737: **Label:** Final changes have not been negotiated in the physician's label with the sponsor. However, class labeling will be included for the EE component and PLLR labeling will be included in Section 8 for Pregnancy and Lactation.
L1738: **Outstanding Nonclinical Issues:** None.
